    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{8\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, which points to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (area under the ROC curve [AUROC] $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Still the same — "for psychometric audits." at the top of page 9.

OK the issue is really stubborn. Let me take the nuclear option: reduce the Discussion §5.2 `\subsection` heading to `\paragraph` to save the vertical space from a subsection heading. Or use `\subsubsection*` instead.

Actually wait, I need to think about this differently. The `\enlargethispage{8\baselineskip}` should be adding quite a lot of space. But the issue is that in two-column mode, both columns need to fill up. The left column has Figure 5 + some text, and the right column has a lot more text. 

The page height is constrained by whichever column fills up first. If the left column is shorter than the right, LaTeX won't stretch the page beyond what the left column can accommodate, even with `\enlargethispage`. So I need to either add more content to the left column or trim the right column down. Let me try shortening the conclusion paragraph by cutting a few words from it.

I've removed "structured" and "under model-family non-independence" to tighten it up.

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage{8\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, which points to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage*{8\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, which points to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

_source line 1343 · 2026-05-25T11:29:54.724Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
`\raggedcolumns` might not be available or it might be causing an error. Let me check the error. Actually, `\raggedcolumns` is a `multicol` package command, not a standard twocolumn command. In standard twocolumn mode, it doesn't exist.

In twocolumn LaTeX, the columns are not balanced mid-document (they're balanced only on the last page). So column balancing shouldn't be the issue on page 8 since it's not the last page.

Actually wait — maybe the issue IS page 8 being the last page of main text. After Conclusion, the next section is Limitations which starts a new page (because of the `\enlargethispage{-6\baselineskip}` in limitations.tex which forces the Limitations page to be shorter). 

Since Limitations uses `\section*`, LaTeX might treat page 8 as the final page and attempt to balance the columns, even though there's an appendix after it. But that shouldn't happen either — two-column balancing only occurs at the document's very end. The real problem is probably that the right column genuinely has one extra line of text, and since both columns must match in height under standard twocolumn mode, no amount of page adjustment can fix it without reducing the left column's content.

My best option is to trim the conclusion down to just two sentences that cover all three contributions concisely, which should force everything to fit properly.
```

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage*{8\\baselineskip}\n\\raggedcolumns\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, which points to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Still "psychometric auditing of LLM benchmarks." at the top of page 9. This is incredibly persistent — every single attempt to trim just one line from the right column of page 8 is failing because LaTeX's line-breaking algorithm still can't fit it.

The fundamental issue is clear: in two-column mode on a non-last page, the columns must be the same height. The left column of page 8 has:
- Figure 5 (matching) at the top
- Start of Discussion §5.1 text

The right column has:
- Continuation of §5.1
- §5.2 audit protocol


- §6 Conclusion

The left column is shorter, so even with `\enlargethispage`, the page height is determined by the left column's content. The right column overflows by exactly 1 line that LaTeX can't fit. I'm considering moving Figure 5 to full-width instead — that way both columns would have roughly equal text content and the figure would sit at the top, which might finally break the column balance deadlock.

But I'm realizing there's a tension here: moving Figure 5 to the appendix would solve the layout problem, but it directly contradicts what the user asked for, which was to improve its visibility. The real issue might be simpler — the figure's `[t]` placement forces it to the top of the column before any text can appear, so what if I add the Discussion section heading above it in the left column to anchor some content before the figure floats up?

Actually, let me try changing the figure placement from `[t]` to `[b]` so it sinks to the bottom of the left column instead. That would push the Discussion text above it, creating better balance between the columns and still keeping the figure visible on the page.

But wait, I'm realizing the real issue might be different. The `\enlargethispage` command in discussion.tex could be applying to page 7 instead of page 8 if the Discussion section starts near the bottom of page 7. Let me check what's actually on page 7 first.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Looking at page 7: "5 Discussion" section header and "5.1 Temporal DIF as Structured Signal" appear at the very bottom of the right column on page 7. The `\enlargethispage` command is BEFORE `\section{Discussion}`, so it's processed while LaTeX is still building page 7. This means the `\enlargethispage*{8\baselineskip}` is being applied to page 7, NOT page 8!

That's the root cause. The `\enlargethispage` is enlarging page 7 instead of page 8. I need to move it so it's applied on page 8 instead.


The solution is to place `\enlargethispage*{8\baselineskip}` in the conclusion.tex file where it'll be processed on page 8, possibly combining it with the existing `\vspace{-6pt}` adjustment before the Conclusion section.
```

    "originalFile": "\\enlargethispage*{8\\baselineskip}\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, which points to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",

- Continuation of Discussion §5.1 text
- "5.2 Recommended DIF Audit Protocol"
- "6 Conclusion" header

And looking at the very bottom of the right column on page 8, I can see:


The conclusion wraps up the findings—across six benchmarks, 17.9–31.3% of items show temporal DIF while rankings stay stable, and the paper confirms the signal isn't just contamination through base-vs-instruct reversals and web-overlap independence. The methodological toolkit (cross-subject matching, DEFF correction, and the Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol) provides practical solutions for ongoing psychometric auditing of LLM benchmarks, with just the last few words spilling onto page 9.

The real issue is a column-balancing problem on page 8—the left column ends early at the section heading while the right column extends much further, and even with `\enlargethispage` stretching both columns equally, the left side doesn't have enough content to push the right column down far enough. I need to either trim roughly four words from the conclusion or add content to the left column to rebalance the page.

   - Two-column page height is constrained by the SHORTER column — if left column has less content, right column can't extend further even with `\enlargethispage`
   - `\dbltextfloatsep` controls spacing below full-width (`figure*`) floats; `\textfloatsep` for single-column floats
   - anonymous.4open.science mirrors from GitHub automatically — paper must use anonymous link, git push uses GitHub

     ```latex
     \begin{abstract}
     Large language model (LLM) benchmark scores are routinely compared across model generations, yet whether individual items measure the same construct over time is rarely tested. We apply differential item functioning (DIF) analysis from educational measurement to the METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models on MMLU, replicated across six benchmarks), validated through semi-synthetic injection with a cross-subject matching guarantee. Between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts, 18--31\% of items exhibit large temporal DIF, 45--95$\times$ above random baselines, while aggregate rankings remain near-perfect ($\rho \geq 0.994$). DIF flags are near-independent of web-overlap contamination labels ($\phi = 0.012$), indicating that global exposure and differential functioning are complementary diagnostic dimensions. Base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions, confirming structured capability shifts beyond contamination. We propose a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct audit protocol with empirically grounded thresholds for benchmark maintainers. LLM benchmarks need psychometric monitoring, not just score reporting\footnote{Code and data are available at \url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/irt_equated_bench_regen-2431/}.}.
     \end{abstract}
     ```

        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF as Structured Signal}\n\\label{sec:disc_temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF detects \\emph{that} items function differently across cohorts, not \\emph{why}, by design (\\Cref{sec:cohorts}). Stratifying by model type reveals opposite patterns: base models ($N{=}1{,}409$) are 60.1\\% reference-favoring ($r = -0.570$), while instruct models ($N{=}478$) are 65.6\\% focal-favoring ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$, $p < 10^{-124}$). This reversal reflects structured item-level bias rather than uniform group impact~\\citep{camilli1994methods}, consistent with DIF as a mechanism-agnostic diagnostic; mixed-mechanism injection reproduces the reversal pattern but cannot match the near-zero instruct gradient, which points to non-contamination drivers (\\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion,app:mixed_mechanism}). Composition downsampling ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp) and base-only/instruct-only analyses confirm DIF is not an artifact of cohort structure (\\Cref{app:composition_control,app:baseinst_temporal,app:within_arch}).\n\n\\looseness=-2\nThree features distinguish LLM temporal DIF from educational settings: (1)~C\\% (29.2\\%; cluster bootstrap CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) substantially exceeds typical rates~\\citep{clauser1998dif}; (2)~DIF flags and web-overlap labels are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$); (3)~the base/instruct reversal (odds ratio [OR] $= 4.75$; \\Cref{app:mechanism_discussion}) favors a broader-signal reading over distinct-construct independence.\n\n\\subsection{Recommended DIF Audit Protocol}\n\\label{sec:disc_protocol}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nItem-level properties do not predict DIF susceptibility (AUROC $= 0.60$; \\Cref{app:predictive}), precluding pre-screening. We recommend: (1)~\\emph{Monitor}: run MH-DIF audits when ${\\geq}$500 models accumulate, triggering review when C\\% exceeds $20\\times$ the random-split baseline; (2)~\\emph{Triage}: DIF $\\times$ web-overlap co-occurrence warrants removal, DIF on web-clean items warrants monitoring; (3)~\\emph{Correct}: expert review, not automatic removal~\\citep{camilli1994methods,penfield2007dif} (\\Cref{app:score_inflation}).\n",
        "numLines": 18,

    "type": "file",

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Psychometric Methods for LLM Evaluation.}\n\\looseness=-1\nThe Mantel-Haenszel procedure \\citep{holland1988mh} remains the most widely used DIF detector in educational testing due to its simplicity and well-characterized statistical properties, operating nonparametrically without requiring an explicit item response theory (IRT) model. DIF analysis is a specific instance of measurement invariance testing \\citep{meredith1993invariance}, which examines whether an instrument functions equivalently across groups. IRT adoption in LLM evaluation has accelerated since \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced it to natural language processing: recent work calibrates test sets for discriminability, item selection, and adaptive evaluation. The closest concurrent work is Growing Pains \\citep{habba2026growingpains}, which uses parametric IRT (two-parameter logistic [2PL] / three-parameter logistic [3PL] fixed-parameter calibration) to equate scores across temporal cohorts. MH-DIF is nonparametric and robust to model misspecification, an advantage given poor 2PL fit for MMLU (root mean square error of approximation [RMSEA] $> 0.08$; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}). Growing Pains prescribes how to equate scores after instability is detected; our work diagnoses \\emph{which} items exhibit instability and quantifies severity via ETS classification, providing pre-screened anchor candidates for downstream equating. Their anchor-item selection can directly consume our stable-item set; our finding that 31\\% of items exhibit temporal DIF identifies which items should \\emph{not} serve as anchors. To our knowledge, no prior work applies DIF to benchmark temporal comparability.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection.}\n\\looseness=-1\nA growing family of methods detects benchmark contamination through model behavior or training-data analysis. Membership inference approaches use token-level log-likelihood statistics \\citep{shi2024mink} or statistical anomalies \\citep{dekoninck2024constat} to flag items likely seen during training. A standard validation practice is to compare detector output against web-overlap labels \\citep{li2024contamination}, which classify items based on verbatim matches in publicly accessible corpora. Such labels measure \\emph{global exposure} (whether an item exists in training data) rather than \\emph{differential item functioning}: whether an item behaves differently across model cohorts. This global-vs-differential distinction motivates our comparison with web-overlap labels (\\Cref{sec:groundtruth}). \\citet{wang2026fragility} show that existing contamination detectors are fragile under reinforcement learning, further motivating behavioral approaches grounded in psychometric theory.\n\n\\paragraph{Benchmark Quality and Design.}\n\\looseness=-1\nMeta-evaluation of static benchmark quality~\\citep{zhu2026bhi} assesses properties at a single snapshot; our temporal DIF analysis diagnoses \\emph{when} measurement properties shift. Work on LLM memorization \\citep{carlini2022quantifying} establishes that memorization depends on data frequency and model scale, reinforcing the distinction between global exposure and differential behavior. MMLU-CF~\\citep{zhao2025mmlucf} reconstructs contamination-free items without replacement; DIF monitoring identifies which existing items remain measurement-invariant. Dynamic benchmarks~\\citep{kiela2021dynabench} mitigate contamination by construction but lack psychometric equating. Broader surveys~\\citep{chang2024survey} identify benchmark saturation as a systemic challenge that DIF analysis directly addresses.\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../paper/sections/related_work.tex"
  },

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "889bd428-049c-476b-a420-92be349b783e",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {

    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\setlength{\\dbltextfloatsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}\n\\setlength{\\textfloatsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nA Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \\geq 100$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 (under uniform injection) and area under the curve (AUC) of 0.93--0.94 for $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ ($= \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{injected}} - \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{baseline}}$) increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$ under binary injection (a calibration upper bound; \\Cref{sec:injection}); MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF: ${\\sim}$29\\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[29.2\\%, 37.8\\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$; \\Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2\\% and weighted-mean yields 13.5\\%, bracketing C\\% at 13.5--29.2\\%; the cluster bootstrap CI excludes the weighted-mean estimate, providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound (\\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation,app:icc_family}). Random permutations within the 2023 cohort confirm the ETS~C threshold maintains per-item FPR below 1\\% (99th percentile $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| = 1.45$); no non-dominant family drives the signal (leave-one-family-out [LOFO] $|\\Delta\\mathrm{C\\%}| \\leq 2.93$\\,pp; \\Cref{tab:lofo,app:dedup_k}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\\% of 0.62\\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\\% (2024; 100 permutations), 24--50$\\times$ below temporal C\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$; at most ${\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). DIF is item-specific: domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (\\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($r = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The temporal DIF finding persists across thresholds ($18.4\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. C\\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \\citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\\ labels are not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & C\\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,090 / ${\\sim}$2,090 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2024 (sanity) & ${\\sim}$400 / ${\\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}\n\\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\\ random item removal. DIF-ordered removal (orange) degrades $\\rho$ faster than random (blue band = mean $\\pm$ 1\\,SD, 20 seeds). Dashed lines: Monitor/Flag (15\\%) and Flag/Act (25\\%) thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).}\n\\label{fig:rho_degradation}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; $p = 0.88$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Label-independent evidence further supports mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$; \\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning}---complementary diagnostic dimensions (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$; cross-subject C\\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\\% converges to ${\\sim}$28--29\\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ (\\Cref{fig:matching}). The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \\leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$---still insufficient to account for the observed 31\\% C\\% (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\\sim}$20\\% (\\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{fig:matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nStable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\\rho = 0.997$, mean $\\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\\leq 2.9\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average (\\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks---including ARC (AI2 Reasoning Challenge)---consistently higher than procedural ones (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ vs.\\ $17.9$--$18.9\\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks (2023 vs.\\ 2024 cohorts). $\\rho$/$\\tau$: rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip: pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio: C\\%/random baseline.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details,app:fig_gsm1k}).\n",
        "numLines": 119,

        "totalLines": 119
      }

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0520_2/projects/irt_equated_bench_regen/paper/sections/limitations.tex",
        "content": "\\raggedbottom\n\\enlargethispage{-6\\baselineskip}\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree primary limitations apply. (1)~The semi-synthetic framework uses uniform binary response flipping, a stylized injection model; reported $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ values may overestimate detection power. Partial memorization scenarios (where models recall item cues without deterministic answer flipping) would produce weaker and qualitatively different DIF patterns than our binary injection model. The framework detects \\emph{that} items function differently but not \\emph{why}: multiple exclusion analyses (ability matching, web-overlap comparison, item-quality annotation) narrow the space of explanations but do not isolate a single mechanism; the specific training factors driving temporal DIF remain unidentified. Follow-up expert review is needed to determine whether flagged items should be retired or retained. (2)~The MH $\\chi^2$ test assumes independent examinees; LLM model families violate this assumption. Two DEFF estimators (13.5--29.2\\%) and family-level cluster bootstrap (95\\% CI: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$) bound the impact, but the extreme relatedness of some families (e.g., hundreds of Llama-2 fine-tunes) exceeds the clustering levels studied in educational settings~\\citep{french2010hierarchical}.\n\n\\looseness=-1\n(3)~Cross-subject matching assumes contamination affects a minority of domains; $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to 0.167 when contamination spans ${\\geq}20$ subjects. Although composition control experiments show a small residual shift ($\\Delta = 1.08$\\,pp; \\Cref{app:composition_control}), base/instruct ratio changes between cohorts remain a potential confound that cannot be fully eliminated without single-architecture longitudinal data. Temporal cohorts defined by calendar year provide coarse granularity, and generalizability to partial-credit and free-form generation benchmarks is untested. Multidimensional DIF~\\citep{ackerman1992didmf} may arise when MMLU's 57 subjects span distinct latent dimensions; cross-subject matching mitigates between-domain multidimensionality but not within-domain violations. IRT parameters are used only for auxiliary power analysis; all core DIF results are nonparametric. Additionally, model release dates need not align with training-data cutoff dates, and generalization beyond multiple-choice question (MCQ) format benchmarks requires further validation, though GSM8K (open-ended) shows consistent patterns.\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },

  },

478

Direct comparison of DIF flags against the
web-overlap labels of Li et al. (2024) on 9,229
annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment = 0.964, Jaccard = 0.206, ϕ = 0.012;
p = 0.88; Table 1 and section C): DIF-C rates are
30.7% among web-contaminated items and 31.8%
among clean items. Because web-overlap labels
are a proxy for training-set membership (based on
verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora),
this near-independence could partly reflect proxy
noise. Label-independent evidence further supports
mechanism separation: base and instruct models
exhibit opposite DIF directions (χ2 = 562.7;
Section 5.1), a reversal incompatible with uniform
contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms
detection power (∆TPR = +0.508 at p = 0.5;
Figure 3). Web-overlap labels measure global exposure, while DIF measures differential functioning—
complementary diagnostic dimensions (Figure 2).

479

4.4

480

Two structural controls and a sensitivity check
fail to reduce temporal C% below 31% (Table 5):
matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C%
= 38.1%; cross-subject C% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT
θ-conditioning (r(θ, total score) = 0.9986). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7% with
non-uniform DIF (|βinteraction | ≥ 0.5), reaching
a union of 47% (Section C). Ability-matched C%
converges to ∼28–29% for both temporal and
within-family comparisons despite vastly different
unmatched rates (Section C).

Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). - [info] [叙事一致性] Introduction ¶1 极度密集：一段内引入5个核心概念(measurement invariance, DIF, temporal score incomparability, MH-DIF, ETS classification)，并给出主要发现(31% C%)和对照(0.62% baseline, ρ≈0.997)。功能上可行但审稿人可能觉得信息过载。
- [warning] [叙事一致性] Introduction ¶1 报告 ρ ≈ 0.997（MMLU-specific），Abstract 和 Conclusion 报告 ρ ≥ 0.994（跨6个benchmark最小值）。两者在各自语境中准确，但读者如果只看 Intro 会高估跨 benchmark 的 ranking 稳定性。建议 Intro 中明确标注"on MMLU"。
- [info] [章节结构] Abstract 回答了四个必答问题（问题/方法/结果/意义），~140 词，简洁高效。
- [info] [章节结构] Introduction 采用压缩3段结构（¶1背景+问题+结果, ¶2 gap, ¶3贡献+控制）而非标准5段。对信息密度大的论文可行，但非典型。

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	\looseness=-1
5	When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \emph{temporal score incomparability} as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected: scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, we find that 31\% of Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\rho \approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by ${\pm}561$ positions at the 99th percentile (\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). This instability does not invalidate the benchmark (aggregate rankings are massively redundant) but it is diagnostic: it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations.
6	
7	Existing approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations (\emph{measurement invariance}) has not been systematically examined (\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.
8	
9	\begin{figure*}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}
12	\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \textbf{\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\ 2024 focal). \textbf{\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \textbf{\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \textbf{\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 31.3\% of items flagged as C (29.2\% after DEFF correction), yet stable-item $\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}
13	\label{fig:pipeline}
14	\end{figure*}
15	
16	We apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]
18	\item \textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 31.3\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\% across six benchmarks; 29.2\% after DEFF correction), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\chi^2 = 562.7$), consistent with mechanism separation (\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).
19	\item \textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).
20	\item \textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
```

1	\setlength{\dbltextfloatsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}
2	\setlength{\textfloatsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}
3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}
5	
6	We apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \Cref{sec:gsm8k}.
7	
8	\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}
9	\label{sec:validation}
10	
11	\looseness=-1
12	A Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \geq 100$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 (under uniform injection) and area under the curve (AUC) of 0.93--0.94 for $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.4$ (\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).
13	
14	\begin{figure}[t]
15	\centering
16	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}
17	\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\Delta\mathrm{TPR}$ ($= \mathrm{TPR}_{\text{injected}} - \mathrm{TPR}_{\text{baseline}}$) increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$ under binary injection (a calibration upper bound; \Cref{sec:injection}); MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0$.}
18	\label{fig:dose_response}
19	\end{figure}
20	
21	\subsection{Temporal DIF: ${\sim}$29\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}
22	\label{sec:temporal}
23	
24	\looseness=-1
25	We compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\% CI: $[29.2\%, 37.8\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\%, 53.2\%]$; \Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2\% and weighted-mean yields 13.5\%, bracketing C\% at 13.5--29.2\%; the cluster bootstrap CI excludes the weighted-mean estimate, providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound (\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation,app:icc_family}). Random permutations within the 2023 cohort confirm the ETS~C threshold maintains per-item FPR below 1\% (99th percentile $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| = 1.45$); no non-dominant family drives the signal (leave-one-family-out [LOFO] $|\Delta\mathrm{C\%}| \leq 2.93$\,pp; \Cref{tab:lofo,app:dedup_k}).
26	
27	\looseness=-1
28	Three controls confirm genuine temporal change (\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\% of 0.62\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\% (2024; 100 permutations), 24--50$\times$ below temporal C\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\% $= 28.9\% \pm 0.1\%$; at most ${\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \Cref{app:method_details}).
29	
30	\looseness=-1
31	Across all 57 subjects, C\% ranges from 20.7\% to 48.4\% (mean $\approx 30\%$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\%). DIF is item-specific: domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\%$ of item-level DIF variance (\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($r = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The temporal DIF finding persists across thresholds ($18.4\%$ at $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 2.0$, $10.2\%$ at $\geq 2.5$).
32	
33	\begin{table}[t]
34	\centering
35	\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\Delta_{\mathrm{MH}}| \geq 1.5$. C\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\ labels are not applicable.}
36	\label{tab:temporal_landscape}
37	\small
38	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
39	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
40	\toprule
41	Cohort Definition & $N_{\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\% & C\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\
42	\midrule
43	Temporal (2023 vs.\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\
44	Ability quintile (Q1--2 vs.\ Q4--5) & ${\sim}$2,090 / ${\sim}$2,090 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\
45	Within-family (Llama-2 vs.\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\
46	Random split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\
47	Random split 2024 (sanity) & ${\sim}$400 / ${\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\
48	Within-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\
49	\bottomrule
50	\end{tabular}}
51	\end{table}
52	
53	\begin{figure*}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}
56	\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\ random item removal. DIF-ordered removal (orange) degrades $\rho$ faster than random (blue band = mean $\pm$ 1\,SD, 20 seeds). Dashed lines: Monitor/Flag (15\%) and Flag/Act (25\%) thresholds (\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).}
57	\label{fig:rho_degradation}
58	\end{figure*}
59	
60	\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}
61	\label{sec:groundtruth}
62	
63	\looseness=-2
64	Direct comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\phi = 0.012$; $p = 0.88$; \Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Label-independent evidence further supports mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\chi^2 = 562.7$; \Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \emph{differential functioning}---complementary diagnostic dimensions (\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).
65	
66	\subsection{Structural Analysis}
67	\label{sec:structural}
68	
69	\looseness=-1
70	Two structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\% below 31\% (\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\% $= 38.1\%$; cross-subject C\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\theta$-conditioning ($r(\theta, \mathrm{total\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\beta_{\mathrm{interaction}}| \geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\% (\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\% converges to ${\sim}$28--29\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\Cref{app:structural}).
71	
72	
73	\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}
74	\label{sec:semisynthetic}
75	
76	\looseness=-1
77	Cross-subject matching achieves $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; \Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ (\Cref{fig:matching}). The $\Delta\mathrm{FPR} \approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\Delta\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$---still insufficient to account for the observed 31\% C\% (\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Difficulty-dependent injection yields $\Delta\mathrm{TPR} \approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\sim}$20\% (\Cref{app:method_details}).
78	
79	\begin{figure}[t]
80	\centering
81	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}
82	\caption{Cross-subject vs.\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\Delta\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}
83	\label{fig:matching}
84	\end{figure}
85	
86	\subsection{Downstream Impact}
87	\label{sec:downstream}
88	
89	\looseness=-2
90	Stable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\rho = 0.997$, mean $\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\leq 2.9\%$; \Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\,pp on average (\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).
91	
92	\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}
93	\label{sec:gsm8k}
94	
95	\looseness=-2
96	Temporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\% ranges from 17.9\% (HellaSwag~\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks---including ARC (AI2 Reasoning Challenge)---consistently higher than procedural ones (C\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\%$ vs.\ $17.9$--$18.9\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.
97	
98	\begin{table}[t]
99	\centering
100	\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks (2023 vs.\ 2024 cohorts). $\rho$/$\tau$: rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip: pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio: C\%/random baseline.}
101	\label{tab:cross_benchmark}
102	\small
103	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
104	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}
105	\toprule
106	Benchmark & Items & C\% & $\rho$ & $\tau$ & Flip\% & Rand.\ C\% & Ratio \\
107	\midrule
108	MMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\times$ \\
109	WinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\times$ \\
110	TruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\times$ \\
111	ARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\times$ \\
112	GSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\times$ \\
113	HellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\times$ \\

117	
118	Semi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\Delta\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \Cref{app:gsm8k_details,app:fig_gsm1k}).

      "content": "\\setlength{\\dbltextfloatsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}\n\\setlength{\\textfloatsep}{8pt plus 2pt minus 2pt}\n\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe apply MH-DIF to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench} (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}), with temporal cohorts defined by release date (2023 vs.\\ 2024). Cross-benchmark validation across all six METABENCH benchmarks appears in \\Cref{sec:gsm8k}.\n\n\\subsection{Validation: DIF Detection Power}\n\\label{sec:validation}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nA Monte Carlo power analysis (1,000 replications) using empirical 2PL parameters from PSN-IRT~\\citep{zhou2026psnirt} ($\\bar{a} = 1.2$, $\\bar{b} = -0.84$) confirms that at $N \\geq 100$ per cohort, ETS~C achieves TPR of 0.68--0.80 (under uniform injection) and area under the curve (AUC) of 0.93--0.94 for $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.4$ (\\Cref{app:method_details}). Our temporal cohorts ($N_{\\mathrm{ref}} = 1{,}080$, $N_{\\mathrm{focal}} = 807$) far exceed this threshold; power varies with item discrimination (TPR 0.40--0.95 across $a = 0.5$--$2.0$; \\Cref{app:power_sensitivity}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_2_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Semi-synthetic dose-response for DIF detection on MMLU. $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR}$ ($= \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{injected}} - \\mathrm{TPR}_{\\text{baseline}}$) increases monotonically with exploitation rate $p$ under binary injection (a calibration upper bound; \\Cref{sec:injection}); MH-DIF is sensitive to differential item behavior when present. Baseline (no injection) produces $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0$.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Temporal DIF: ${\\sim}$29\\% of Items Exhibit Differential Functioning}\n\\label{sec:temporal}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWe compare 2023 ($N = 1{,}080$) against 2024 ($N = 807$) models; 31.3\\% of items receive ETS~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$; bootstrap 95\\% CI: $[29.2\\%, 37.8\\%]$; family-level cluster bootstrap: $[26.7\\%, 53.2\\%]$; \\Cref{app:uncertainty}). Two DEFF estimators bound the non-independence effect: simple-mean DEFF yields 29.2\\% and weighted-mean yields 13.5\\%, bracketing C\\% at 13.5--29.2\\%; the cluster bootstrap CI excludes the weighted-mean estimate, providing model-independent evidence that over-correction drives the lower bound (\\Cref{app:deff,app:deff_simulation,app:icc_family}). Random permutations within the 2023 cohort confirm the ETS~C threshold maintains per-item FPR below 1\\% (99th percentile $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| = 1.45$); no non-dominant family drives the signal (leave-one-family-out [LOFO] $|\\Delta\\mathrm{C\\%}| \\leq 2.93$\\,pp; \\Cref{tab:lofo,app:dedup_k}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nThree controls confirm genuine temporal change (\\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}): random splits yield mean C\\% of 0.62\\% (2023; 100 permutations) and 1.3\\% (2024; 100 permutations), 24--50$\\times$ below temporal C\\%; ability-quintile comparison yields 2.9\\%; and 1:1 nearest-neighbor matching on total score ($N{=}638$/group, Cohen's $d = -0.17$) yields C\\% $= 28.9\\% \\pm 0.1\\%$; at most ${\\sim}2.4$~pp is attributable to ability mismatch ($p < 0.01$; \\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\looseness=-1\nAcross all 57 subjects, C\\% ranges from 20.7\\% to 48.4\\% (mean $\\approx 30\\%$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape}). DIF rates are domain-invariant (Kruskal-Wallis $p = 0.78$), but DIF \\emph{direction} exhibits domain dependence: STEM items favor 2024 models (55.4\\%), Humanities favor 2023 (64.4\\%). DIF is item-specific: domain-level accuracy gains explain ${<}0.1\\%$ of item-level DIF variance (\\Cref{app:structural}). A difficulty--direction interaction emerges ($r = -0.597$): 2024 models gain on hard items while eroding on easy ones. The temporal DIF finding persists across thresholds ($18.4\\%$ at $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 2.0$, $10.2\\%$ at $\\geq 2.5$).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{DIF landscape across cohort definitions. ETS~C denotes the percentage of items with $|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$. C\\% (web-clean) is the DIF-C flagging rate among items classified as web-clean by \\citet{li2024contamination}. Enrichment is the ratio of observed to expected overlap between DIF flags and web-overlap labels. Dashes (--) indicate comparisons where Li et al.\\ labels are not applicable.}\n\\label{tab:temporal_landscape}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\nCohort Definition & $N_{\\mathrm{ref}}$ / $N_{\\mathrm{focal}}$ & C\\% & C\\% (web-clean) & Enrich. \\\\\n\\midrule\nTemporal (2023 vs.\\ 2024) & 1,080 / 807 & 31.3 & 0.318 & 0.964 \\\\\nAbility quintile (Q1--2 vs.\\ Q4--5) & ${\\sim}$2,090 / ${\\sim}$2,090 & 2.9 & 0.027 & 1.19 \\\\\nWithin-family (Llama-2 vs.\\ 3) & 161 / 308 & 61.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2023 (sanity) & 540 / 540 & 0.62 & -- & -- \\\\\nRandom split 2024 (sanity) & ${\\sim}$400 / ${\\sim}$400 & 1.3 & -- & -- \\\\\nWithin-subject (avg, 5 subj.) & \\textit{varies} & 38.1 & -- & -- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_A3_rho_degradation.pdf}\n\\caption{Ranking degradation under DIF-ordered vs.\\ random item removal. DIF-ordered removal (orange) degrades $\\rho$ faster than random (blue band = mean $\\pm$ 1\\,SD, 20 seeds). Dashed lines: Monitor/Flag (15\\%) and Flag/Act (25\\%) thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).}\n\\label{fig:rho_degradation}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Web-Overlap Labels}\n\\label{sec:groundtruth}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nDirect comparison of DIF flags against the web-overlap labels of \\citet{li2024contamination} on 9,229 annotated items shows near-independence (enrichment $= 0.964$, Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$; $p = 0.88$; \\Cref{tab:temporal_landscape,app:structural}): DIF-C rates are 30.7\\% among web-contaminated items and 31.8\\% among clean items. Because web-overlap labels are a proxy for training-set membership (based on verbatim web matches, not actual training corpora), this near-independence could partly reflect proxy noise. Label-independent evidence further supports mechanism separation: base and instruct models exhibit opposite DIF directions ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$; \\Cref{sec:disc_temporal}), a reversal incompatible with uniform contamination. Semi-synthetic injection confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.508$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{fig:dose_response}). Web-overlap labels measure \\emph{global exposure}, while DIF measures \\emph{differential functioning}---complementary diagnostic dimensions (\\Cref{fig:global_vs_differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Structural Analysis}\n\\label{sec:structural}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nTwo structural controls and a sensitivity check fail to reduce temporal C\\% below 31\\% (\\Cref{tab:structural_comparison}): matched-ability quintiles, within-subject DIF (C\\% $= 38.1\\%$; cross-subject C\\% is more conservative due to lower local dependence), and IRT $\\theta$-conditioning ($r(\\theta, \\mathrm{total\\ score}) = 0.9986$). Logistic regression detects an additional 17.7\\% with non-uniform DIF ($|\\beta_{\\mathrm{interaction}}| \\geq 0.5$), reaching a union of 47\\% (\\Cref{app:structural}). Ability-matched C\\% converges to ${\\sim}$28--29\\% for both temporal and within-family comparisons despite vastly different unmatched rates (\\Cref{app:structural}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Detection Power under Controlled Injection}\n\\label{sec:semisynthetic}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nCross-subject matching achieves $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$ across all five MMLU subjects (4/5 with $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ at $p = 0.5$; \\Cref{sec:injection}), while within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ (\\Cref{fig:matching}). The $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} \\approx 0$ guarantee holds for $k \\leq 5$ contaminated subjects ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} < 0.007$); at $k{=}20$, $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR}$ rises to $0.167$---still insufficient to account for the observed 31\\% C\\% (\\Cref{tab:multi_subject_injection}). Difficulty-dependent injection yields $\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} \\approx 0.41$ at $p = 0.5$ (vs.\\ $0.51$ uniform), bounding overestimation at ${\\sim}$20\\% (\\Cref{app:method_details}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_3_within_subject_matching.pdf}\n\\caption{Cross-subject vs.\\ within-subject matching for semi-synthetic DIF detection. Cross-subject matching eliminates FPR inflation ($\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0.000$) while preserving detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} > 0.3$ for 4/5 subjects at $p = 0.5$). Within-subject matching inflates FPR by ${+}0.186$ on average.}\n\\label{fig:matching}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Downstream Impact}\n\\label{sec:downstream}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nStable-item ranks are preserved across all six benchmarks (mean $\\rho = 0.997$, mean $\\tau = 0.962$; flip rate $\\leq 2.9\\%$; \\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}). DIF-flagged items concentrate discriminative signal: DIF-ordered removal degrades $\\rho$ faster than random removal across all six benchmarks (\\Cref{fig:rho_degradation}; $z = -8$ to $-49$; \\Cref{app:fig_downstream}). Removing C-flagged items disproportionately disadvantages 2024 models (cohort gap $= 96.2$ positions, $40\\times$ random; $p < 0.001$); DIF-corrected scoring shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average (\\Cref{app:score_inflation,app:rank_reversal}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Benchmark Validation}\n\\label{sec:gsm8k}\n\n\\looseness=-2\nTemporal DIF extends across all six METABENCH benchmarks (\\Cref{tab:cross_benchmark}): C\\% ranges from 17.9\\% (HellaSwag~\\citep{zellers2019hellaswag}) to 31.3\\% (MMLU), with knowledge/reasoning benchmarks---including ARC (AI2 Reasoning Challenge)---consistently higher than procedural ones (C\\% $= 26.9$--$31.3\\%$ vs.\\ $17.9$--$18.9\\%$). These differences may partly reflect item difficulty distributions; we treat them as descriptive.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Temporal DIF across six METABENCH benchmarks (2023 vs.\\ 2024 cohorts). $\\rho$/$\\tau$: rank correlation between full and stable-item rankings; Flip: pairwise rank-flip rate; Ratio: C\\%/random baseline.}\n\\label{tab:cross_benchmark}\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrl}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Items & C\\% & $\\rho$ & $\\tau$ & Flip\\% & Rand.\\ C\\% & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & 12{,}508 & 31.3 & 0.9965 & 0.9600 & 2.00 & 0.62 & 50$\\times$ \\\\\nWinoGrande & 1{,}267 & 30.3 & 0.9943 & 0.9432 & 2.84 & 0.32 & 95$\\times$ \\\\\nTruthfulQA & 817 & 29.7 & 0.9971 & 0.9586 & 2.07 & 0.37 & 80$\\times$ \\\\\nARC & 1{,}172 & 26.9 & 0.9962 & 0.9538 & 2.31 & 0.60 & 45$\\times$ \\\\\nGSM8K & 1{,}319 & 18.9 & 0.9993 & 0.9843 & 0.78 & 0.30 & 63$\\times$ \\\\\nHellaSwag & 10{,}042 & 17.9 & 0.9986 & 0.9722 & 1.39 & 0.28 & 64$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nSemi-synthetic injection on GSM8K confirms detection power ($\\Delta\\mathrm{TPR} = {+}0.514$; \\Cref{app:gsm8k_details,app:fig_gsm1k}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\looseness=-1\nWhen we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected: scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause. Applying Mantel-Haenszel DIF analysis~\\citep{holland1988mh} with Educational Testing Service (ETS) severity classification to the METABENCH response matrix~\\citep{kipnis2024metabench}, we find that 31\\% of Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMLU)~\\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu} items receive ETS Category~C classification ($|\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}| \\geq 1.5$, $p < 0.05$) between 2023 and 2024 model cohorts---versus 0.62\\% under a random-split control. Aggregate rankings are robust ($\\rho \\approx 0.997$), but absolute scores are not directly comparable: DIF-corrected scoring (excluding all C-flagged items) shifts accuracy by ${+}2.12$\\,pp on average, with individual models displaced by ${\\pm}561$ positions at the 99th percentile (\\Cref{app:rank_reversal}). This instability does not invalidate the benchmark (aggregate rankings are massively redundant) but it is diagnostic: it reveals which capability dimensions shift across model generations.\n\nExisting approaches to benchmark integrity focus on \\emph{global exposure}---whether items appear in training corpora---through membership inference~\\citep{shi2024mink} and performance-based detection~\\citep{dekoninck2024constat,liang2026dvd,tao2026selfcritique}, validated against web-overlap labels~\\citep{li2024contamination}. Whether benchmark items function equivalently across model generations (\\emph{measurement invariance}) has not been systematically examined (\\Cref{fig:pipeline,fig:global_vs_differential}). We find these two signals are near-independent (Jaccard $= 0.206$, $\\phi = 0.012$): complementary diagnostic dimensions, not competing ones.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_0_pipeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Four-stage psychometric auditing pipeline. \\textbf{\\ding{192}} A binary response matrix records model correctness across benchmark items, with models split into temporal cohorts (2023 reference vs.\\ 2024 focal). \\textbf{\\ding{193}} Models are stratified into $K{=}5$ score quintiles. \\textbf{\\ding{194}} Per-item Mantel-Haenszel DIF tests compute $\\Delta_{\\mathrm{MH}}$ across strata. \\textbf{\\ding{195}} ETS classification assigns severity labels (A/B/C). Result: 31.3\\% of items flagged as C (29.2\\% after DEFF correction), yet stable-item $\\rho = 0.997$, replicated across six benchmarks.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe apply a temporal DIF audit framework to the full METABENCH response matrix (5,227 models $\\times$ 12,508 MMLU items), to our knowledge the largest-scale DIF analysis on LLM benchmarks, complementing concurrent work on anchor-item calibration~\\citep{habba2026growingpains} and motivated by the fragility of existing detectors under post-training perturbation~\\citep{wang2026fragility}. Two structural controls, a sensitivity check, and replication across all six METABENCH benchmarks confirm that the temporal DIF rate (29.2\\% after non-independence correction; 17.9--31.3\\% across benchmarks) is not a methodological artifact (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:structural,sec:gsm8k}). We make three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*,topsep=2pt]\n\\item \\textbf{Temporal DIF at scale}: 31.3\\% of MMLU items exhibit temporal DIF (17.9--31.3\\% across six benchmarks; 29.2\\% after DEFF correction), near-independent of web-overlap labels (Jaccard $= 0.206$); base-vs-instruct stratification reveals opposite patterns ($\\chi^2 = 562.7$), consistent with mechanism separation (\\Cref{sec:temporal,sec:groundtruth,sec:gsm8k,sec:disc_temporal}).\n\\item \\textbf{Cross-subject matching with $\\Delta\\mathrm{FPR} = 0$ guarantee}: solves matching-variable corruption under single-subject contamination, extending to $k \\leq 5$; complemented by design effect (DEFF) correction and semi-synthetic validation (\\Cref{sec:semisynthetic}).\n\\item \\textbf{Actionable audit protocol}: a three-step Monitor/Triage/Correct protocol with empirically grounded thresholds (\\Cref{sec:disc_protocol}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

          "-When we say ``Model B outperforms Model A on MMLU,'' we implicitly assume that the benchmark measures the same construct in the same way for both models. This assumption of temporal comparability underpins every cross-generation comparison published on leaderboards and in model reports, yet it is rarely tested. We use \\emph{measurement invariance} to denote the property that test items function identically across groups~\\citep{meredith1993invariance}, \\emph{differential item functioning} (DIF) as the item-level statistical test for violations, and \\emph{temporal score incomparability} as the empirical phenomenon when DIF is detected: scores are not directly comparable across cohorts because item measurement properties differ systematically, without implying degradation or attributing cause.
